1	% conclusion.tex — D073 Phase 2 revision (page-compressed)
2	
3	\section{Discussion}
4	\label{sec:discussion}
5	
6	\paragraph{Confounds and transferability.}
7	Three observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\,=\,0.541$\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\,$\geq$\,0.97). ToxicChat (+18--42\,pp; Table~\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer; mixed-LLM 9-class maintains DD F1\,=\,0.818$\pm$0.096 while MLM collapses (Appendix~\ref{app:mixed_llm}). Composition appears more determinant than sample count, though partially confounded (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_validation}).
8	
9	\paragraph{Evidence for discriminative compression.}
10	Classification DAPT compresses features into few discriminative dimensions (participation ratio 3.7--9.4 vs.\ 25--32; Fisher 2.6--19.7 vs.\ $<$0.001; Appendix~\ref{app:representation}). Scrambled labels yield comparable compression (Fisher\,=\,2.6), consistent with \citet{zhang2021understanding}, with two findings: (i)~domain-relevant partitioning is necessary (topic DAPT has higher per-turn Fisher yet fails at conversation level; Appendix~\ref{app:topic_failure}), and (ii)~compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12 (Appendix~\ref{app:layerwise}). A discriminative probe over these 3 dimensions achieves perfect AA OOD detection vs.\ MLM 0.636 with 768 (Appendix~\ref{app:discriminative_probe}); frozen probing near-parity (3.1\,pp) is consistent with Stage-1 establishing this structure, though pattern memorization may also contribute.
11	
12	\paragraph{Condition-specific advantage.}
13	The hierarchy is specific to synthetic multi-turn evaluation. On ToxicChat (single-turn human data), JB-MLM leads (0.837) while 9-class ranks lower (0.596); 65\% of the gap stems from false positives (FPR 0.510 vs.\ 0.167), 35\% from missed attacks. Truncation \emph{widens} the 9-class advantage (+6.0\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:truncated}), implicating synthetic-human distributional differences.
14	
15	\paragraph{Generalization boundaries.}
16	At larger scale (RoBERTa-large, 355M), vanilla representations suffice for ActorAttack without adaptation, suggesting scale-dependent DAPT utility. Mean pooling matches BiGRU ($\leq$1\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:temporal}): detection is turn-level anomaly aggregation. Benign-side shift (WildChat FPR 48.1\%$\rightarrow$68.1\%) is mitigated by 10 target conversations (FPR$\rightarrow$0\%; OASST2 1.0\%$\pm$0.5\% at $N$\,=\,25; Appendix~\ref{app:oasst2}).
17	
18	\section{Conclusion}
19	
20	Thirteen encoder configurations on synthetic multi-turn data (IID benign) decompose jailbreak generalization into attack-side (discriminative compression; MLM collapses under mixed-LLM) and benign-side (FPR 65--68\%$\rightarrow$0--1.5\%, $N$\,=\,10--25) axes at 24.3$\times$ speedup; domain-relevant classification suffices ($d\!<\!0.1$; AA $d$\,=\,0.44).
21	
22	\section{Limitations}
23	\label{sec:limitations}
24	
25	All findings derive from synthetic conversations generated by a single LLM (Qwen3-8B; 350 training, 76--118 test per family, 3 seeds); the evidence hierarchy reverses on human single-turn data (Section~\ref{sec:discussion}), and no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists. OOD F1 figures are upper bounds because benign data is IID; benign-side adaptation (WildChat FPR 68.1\%\,$\rightarrow$\,0\% with 10 conversations; OASST2 57.5\%\,$\rightarrow$\,1.0\% at $N$\,=\,25) is validated on only two platforms with small samples ($n \leq 49$). Benign-only adaptation does not address cross-LLM distribution shift: adapting on Llama-origin conversations collapses Llama jailbreak detection (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_llm}). Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\citep{zou2023gcg} and non-text modalities are not addressed.
26	
27	\section*{Ethics Statement}
28	
29	This work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content) and withholding attack-generation prompts. Detection code, model weights, and evaluation data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0. No human subjects were involved.
30	
```

```text
1	% method.tex — D024 compressed
2	
3	\section{Method}
4	\label{sec:method}
5	
6	Cross-attack generalization could stem from domain exposure, classification supervision, label correctness, label granularity, or domain specificity. Disentangling these factors demands an architecture where exactly one component varies across conditions. DisPeD (Disentangled Pattern Detection) separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2); the name reflects this two-stage disentanglement of domain-adapted encoding from downstream classification. Seven Stage-1 variants share the same training data and downstream pipeline but differ solely in their pretraining objective; each isolates a single factor (Figure~\ref{fig:architecture}).
7	
8	\subsection{Problem Formulation}
9	
10	A conversation $C = \{u_1, a_1, \ldots, u_N, a_N\}$ alternates user turns $u_i$ and assistant responses $a_i$. Given a prefix of $K$ user turns, the model predicts $y \in \{0, 1\}$ (jailbreak vs.\ benign). We restrict input to user turns because adversarial intent resides in the attacker's messages. This formulation supports early detection from partial prefixes.
11	
12	\subsection{Stage 1: Turn-Level Encoder Adaptation}
13	
14	Each user turn $u_i$ is encoded by DeBERTa-v3-base~\citep{he2023debertav3}; the \texttt{[CLS]} embedding $h_i \in \mathbb{R}^d$ serves as the turn representation. Seven variants, summarized in Table~\ref{tab:variants}, share the same training data but differ in objective.
15	
16	\begin{table}[t]
17	\centering
18	\small
19	\caption{Stage-1 encoder variants. All operate on the same training conversations except Wikipedia MLM (matched token count on Wikipedia text).}
20	\label{tab:variants}
21	\begin{tabular}{@{}p{1.6cm}p{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}
22	\toprule
23	\textbf{Variant} & \textbf{Objective} & \textbf{Tests whether\ldots} \\
24	\midrule
25	9-class & 9-class persuasion + binary intent & full model \\
26	Binary & binary persuasion + binary intent & granularity matters \\
27	Scrambled & random 9-class + binary intent & label correctness matters \\
28	MLM-only & 15\% token masking & supervision is necessary \\
29	Topic & 5-class topic classif. & any task suffices \\
30	Wiki MLM & 15\% masking on Wikipedia & domain specificity matters \\
31	Vanilla & no training & Stage-2 alone suffices \\
32	\bottomrule
33	\end{tabular}
34	\end{table}
35	
36	The \textbf{9-class} variant uses two classification heads: 9-class persuasion strategy classification ($L_{\text{strategy}}$) and binary jailbreak intent ($L_{\text{intent}}$), with combined loss $L = L_{\text{strategy}} + \alpha \cdot L_{\text{intent}}$ ($\alpha = 0.3$, selected from $\{0, 0.3, 0.5, 2.0\}$; all values yield F1\,=\,1.0 on IID; Appendix~\ref{app:lambda}). The taxonomy comprises eight persuasion strategies (rapport building, authority appeal, emotional manipulation, logical reframing, role assignment, gradual escalation, obfuscation, direct request) plus a none category, grounded in PAP~\citep{zeng2024pap} and Cialdini's principles~\citep{cialdini2001influence}. Strategy annotations are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting that includes definitions and examples for each category; inter-annotator agreement between model annotations and a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\kappa$=0.78.
37	
38	The \textbf{binary} variant collapses the 9-class taxonomy into persuasive vs.\ non-persuasive, testing whether label granularity matters for detection or robustness.
39	The \textbf{scrambled} variant randomly permutes the 9-class labels across turns, preserving classification structure (cross-entropy loss, 9-class output space) while destroying label semantics. This isolates whether the classification objective provides representational benefits independent of label correctness.
40	The \textbf{MLM-only} variant applies 15\% masked language modeling on the same jailbreak conversation text without any classification head, testing whether unsupervised domain exposure suffices.
41	The \textbf{topic} variant trains a 5-class topic classifier (technology, health, finance, education, general) on the same conversations, testing whether \emph{any} classification task provides the same benefit or whether task alignment matters.
42	The \textbf{Wikipedia MLM} variant applies the same MLM objective to a token-matched sample of Wikipedia text; this isolates domain-specific from general continued pretraining.
43	The \textbf{vanilla} baseline uses the pretrained DeBERTa-v3-base weights without any continued pretraining; it measures what Stage~2 alone can achieve.
44	
45	\subsection{Stage 2: Conversation-Level Classification}
46	
47	Turn embeddings $\{h_1, \ldots, h_K\}$ from the frozen Stage-1 encoder are aggregated by a 2-layer bidirectional GRU ($d_{\text{hidden}}$=256, dropout 0.3): $\bar{h} = [\overrightarrow{h_K};\; \overleftarrow{h_1}] \in \mathbb{R}^{512}$.
48	A two-layer MLP ($512 \to 256 \to 2$, ReLU activation) maps $\bar{h}$ to $\hat{y} \in \{0, 1\}$.
49	The Stage-1 encoder is frozen during Stage~2 training; only the BiGRU and MLP parameters are updated.
50	This frozen-encoder design ensures ablation fairness: all seven Stage-1 variants are evaluated under identical downstream conditions, isolating the effect of Stage-1 representation quality from Stage-2 classifier capacity.
51	Three-seed evaluation confirms that mean pooling matches BiGRU performance (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}).
52	
```

```text
1	% related_work.tex — D048 deep writing optimization
2	
3	\section{Related Work}
4	
5	\paragraph{Multi-turn jailbreak attacks.}
6	All multi-turn jailbreak families share a persuasion substrate~\citep{zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,li2024mhj}: Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, FITD~\citep{fitd2024}, DD~\citep{deceptivedelight2024}, and ActorAttack~\citep{ren2024actorattack} each operationalize it differently, while automated methods~\citep{chao2023pair,mehrotra2024tap,perez2022redteaming} iteratively refine prompts and many-shot attacks~\citep{anil2024manyshot} exploit long-context in-context learning. Benchmarks standardize evaluation at single-turn~\citep{mazeika2024harmbench,chao2024jailbreakbench} and multi-turn~\citep{wei2023jailbroken,jiang2024redqueen,song2026multibreak} scales. A detector must generalize across families, not merely succeed on its training distribution.
7	
8	\paragraph{Multi-turn defenses and production safety.}
9	No existing defense evaluates cross-attack transfer. Conversation-level systems (DeepContext~\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\citep{gguard2025}, SafeDream~\citep{safedream2026}, JailDAM~\citep{nian2025jaildam}) and representation-level approaches~\citep{lu2025xboundary,zhang2025jbshield,xie2024gradsafe,zou2024circuitbreakers,kulkarni2026latentadversarial,kadali2026trace,hua2026rcs,shen2025antidote,li2025repejailbreak,wang2026trajguard,templeton2024saemitigators} each evaluate on a single family; the latter require white-box access and can be brittle~\citep{derya2026revisitingjbshield}. J-DAPT~\citep{marchiori2025jdapt} applies CORAL-based domain adaptation with cross-modal fusion for multimodal robotic jailbreak detection; our work focuses on text-only multi-turn persuasion attacks and decomposes which adaptation factors drive cross-attack generalization through controlled ablation. Per-turn classifiers~\citep{inan2023llamaguard,metallamaguard3_2024,zeng2024shieldgemma,han2024wildguard,anthropic2025constitutional} and single-turn defenses~\citep{robey2024smoothllm,xu2024safedecoding,jain2023baseline,alon2023perplexity,phute2023selfdefense,hsu2024improved} miss multi-turn trajectories; M2S~\citep{li2025m2s} converts multi-turn attacks to single-turn as a complementary approach. Our ablation contributes to filling this gap via decomposition across thirteen configurations under OOD evaluation.
10	
11	\paragraph{Computational persuasion and discourse.}
12	Persuasion and discourse frameworks~\citep{austin1962speechacts,searle1969speechacts,mann1988rst,prasad2008pdtb,stolcke2000dialogueact} and technique-level classification~\citep{cialdini2001influence,zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,dasanmartino2019fine,dasanmartino2020semeval,wang2019persuasion} offer interpretable vocabulary for attacks, but whether such structure aids detection has not been tested. Our ablation shows the taxonomy enables characterization, but detection operates through domain-adapted embeddings regardless of label correctness.
13	
14	\paragraph{Domain-adaptive pretraining.}
15	Continued pretraining on domain text yields consistent gains~\citep{gururangan2020dontstop,lee2020biobert,beltagy2019scibert,howard2018ulmfit}, but has not been disentangled for safety-critical generalization. Intermediate-task transfer~\citep{phang2018stilts} and multi-task learning~\citep{caruana1997multitask,liu2019mtdnn} confound domain exposure with supervised signal; our MLM control confirms pretraining alone closes the OOD gap. Dataset artifacts~\citep{gururangan2018artifacts,niven2019probing} motivate TF-IDF, adversarial NLP~\citep{jin2020textfooler,li2020bertattack,nasr2025attackermovessecond,andriushchenko2024adaptive} informs perturbation, and probing~\citep{conneau2018probing,hewitt2019structural} underpins CKA analysis.
16	
```

Now I'm verifying the specific numbers throughout the paper. The conclusion correctly cites that 65% of the gap comes from false positives while 35% comes from missed attacks, which aligns with the experimental data. The abstract now clearly separates the finding that template attacks remain detectable while ActorAttack's structural complexity causes performance degradation. The discussion section has been properly split to address these distinctions.

I'm systematically checking all numerical claims: the 1.8% Llama Guard 3 detection rate aligns with the F1 scores in Table 2, the 0.97–1.0 range for the defended model matches the 9-class results, and the vanilla baseline of 0.26–0.31 is consistent. The ActorAttack degradation to F1=0.504 needs verification against the experimental results. I'm verifying the MLM collapse claim against the experimental data—it shows a drop to 0.289 which matches the vanilla baseline. Now checking the FPR ranges: WildChat hits 68.1% but OASST2 is only 57.5%, so the stated 65–68% range is slightly off and should probably be 58–68% to cover both datasets. The target-domain conversation results check out across both sources, and the directional parameter range is confirmed. I found where the 3.9 percentage point gap comes from—it's the maximum difference between the binary variant at 0.990 and the scrambled variant at 0.951 on the AA metric, which matches the source material exactly. For the conclusion, there's a discrepancy where the 65% lower bound doesn't align with OASST2's actual 57.5%, though the TOST analysis values check out. The limitations section confirms the training and test set sizes, and clarifies that OASST2 shows the correct 57.5%→1.0% improvement at N=25, not the 65% mentioned earlier.

I'm noticing a real inconsistency: the introduction and conclusion both cite a 65–68% pre-adaptation FPR range, but the limitations correctly show OASST2 at 57.5%, which means the range should actually be around 58–68%. The other metrics like the 355M parameter count, 24.3× speedup, and 43× parameter ratio all check out against the experiments.

Now I'm looking at the WildChat FPR notation in the conclusion—the "48.1%→68.1%" phrasing is ambiguous because it's comparing vanilla FPR to 9-class DAPT FPR, which are different model variants rather than a true distribution shift. The intended meaning seems to be that DAPT exacerbates the benign-side shift problem, but the arrow notation makes it unclear whether this represents an actual change or just a comparison between two configurations.

Looking at the broader consistency issues, there's a discrepancy between the introduction and conclusion claiming "65--68%" FPR for the benign-side shift, while the limitations section shows OASST2 at 57.5%—the actual numbers should be WildChat 68.1% and OASST2 57.5%, so the range cited in the intro and conclusion is misleading.

MLM collapses under mixed-LLM) and benign-side (FPR 65--68\\%$\\rightarrow$0--1.5\\%, $N$\\,=\\,10--25) axes at 24.3$\\times$ speedup; domain-relevant classification suffices ($d\\!<\\!0.1$; AA $d$\\,=\\,0.44).\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/conclusion.tex:25:[Omitted long matching line]\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/appendix.tex:112:Wiki MLM & .656$\\pm$.224 & .672$\\pm$.252 & .693$\\pm$.280 & .689$\\pm$.285 & .686$\\pm$.297 \\\\\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/appendix.tex:545:\\paragraph{Second benign source: OASST2.}\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/appendix.tex:546:\\label{app:oasst2}\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/appendix.tex:548:To test whether benign-side shift generalizes beyond WildChat, we evaluate on 200 benign multi-turn conversations from OpenAssistant OASST2~\\citep{kopf2023openassistant}---a crowdsourced dataset of real human conversations, distinct from WildChat (GPT-3.5 user logs) in both authorship and topic distribution.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/appendix.tex:553:\\caption{OASST2 benign adaptation curve: FPR on 200 OASST2 benign conversations and OOD jailbreak detection F1 as a function of $N$ OASST2 conversations added to training. $N$\\,=\\,25 confirmed across 3 seeds (mean$\\pm$std); 2. Conclusion line 20: "FPR 65--68%→0--1.5%, N=10--25"

But the actual numbers are WildChat 9-class FPR = 68.1% and OASST2 9-class FPR = 57.5%, so the range should be closer to 58--68% rather than 65--68%. The Limitations section gets the individual values right, but there's a clear discrepancy where the lower bound is inflated by about 7.5 percentage points compared to what the data actually shows.

I'm checking whether 65% could come from a different variant or seed of WildChat. Looking at the per-seed FPR values for WildChat 9-class—66.8%, 61.1%, 76.5%—the mean is 68.1% and 65% doesn't match any individual seed either. This appears to be a straightforward error in how the range was reported.

Let me trace through the exp51 data more carefully to understand what's happening with the adaptation numbers. I've identified the key fixes that have been applied and spotted one remaining issue with the FPR range—the lower bound of 65% is incorrect since OASST2 actually shows 57.5%, not 65%. This discrepancy appears in multiple places: the introduction, conclusion, and introduction again.

Looking at the page count, the main text runs about 354 lines plus tables and figures, which is quite compressed for a typical ACL submission that usually fits 55-65 lines per page. I'm ready to post the verification report now.

[severity: warning] \"FPR 65--68%\" 下界与 OASST2 数据不一致**\n\n三处出现：\n- `introduction.tex` 第24行（contribution #1）：\"FPR from 65--68% to 0--1.5% on two independent sources (WildChat, OASST2)\"\n- `conclusion.tex` 第20行：\"FPR 65--68%→0--1.5%, N=10--25\"\n- ~~conclusion.tex 第16行~~：此处写的是 \"48.1%→68.1%\"，不同上下文\n\n实际数据（两个独立源的 9-class baseline FPR）：\n- WildChat：68.1%（exp19, 3-seed mean）→ **68%** ✓\n- OASST2：57.5%（exp51, n=200；appendix Table 行559 确认）→ **58%** ✗ 65%\n\n**正确范围应为 \"~58--68%\"，不是 \"65--68%\"**。下界被虚高 ~7.5pp。\n\nLimitations 第25行正确写了 \"OASST2 57.5%→1.0%\"，与 Introduction/Conclusion 矛盾。审稿人交叉核对 Limitations vs Conclusion 会发现此不一致。\n\n**建议修正**：将 Introduction contribution #1 和 Conclusion 中的 \"65--68%\" 改为 \"58--68%\" 或更保守的 \"up to 68%\"。\n\n---\n\n### 7. 页面预算\n\n主文件行数（不含 appendix/checklist）：\n- abstract(10) + intro(29) + method(51) + experiments(220) + related_work(15) + conclusion/discussion/limitations/ethics(29) = ~354行\n- 含 4 个 table + 2 个 figure\n- Limitations + Ethics 不计入 8 页\n\n无法编译验证，但内容已高度压缩，估计在 8 页边缘。**建议编译后确认**。\n\n---\n\n### 全文数字交叉验证结果\n\n| 数字 | 来源 | 论文 | 一致 |\n|------|------|------|------|\n| Vanilla DD/AA Full | Table 2, exp data | 0.26--0.31 | ✅ |\n| 9-class DD/AA Full | Table 2 | 0.97--1.0 | ✅ |\n| AA mixed-LLM | experiments.tex | 0.504 | ✅ |\n| Speedup | exp46 | 24.3× | ✅ |\n| Memory ratio | exp46 | 18.5× | ✅ |\n| ToxicChat FP/FN | exp63 | 65%/35% | ✅ |\n| FPR 9class/MLM | exp63 | 0.510/0.167 | ✅ |\n| WildChat FPR | exp19 | 68.1% | ✅ |\n| OASST2 baseline FPR | exp51 | 57.5% | ✅ |\n| OASST2 adapted FPR | exp51 curve | 1.0%±0.5% @N=25 | ✅ |\n| FPR range \"65--68%\" | intro/concl | ⚠️ 应为 58--68% | ❌ |\n| TOST d values | exp11/exp41 | d<0.1, d=0.44 | ✅ |\n| Scrambled gap | Table 2 | 3.9pp max | ✅ |\n\n**修正 \"65--68%\" 后可提交 R18。**",

}

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"path\": \"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/paper_framing.md\", \"content\": \"# Paper Framing — persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect\\n\\n## Target Venue\\n- Primary: EMNLP 2026 Main (ARR submission)\\n- Deadline: 2026-05-25 (ARR May cycle)\\n- Track: LLM Security / Discourse and Pragmatics\\n- Commitment deadline: 2026-08-02\\n\\n## Title (Working)\\nWhat Makes Multi-Turn Jailbreaks Detectable? A Systematic Decomposition of Cross-Attack Generalization\\n\\n## Core Insight (D030 → D049 — classification-based DAPT reframe)\\nDomain adaptation universally improves cross-attack generalization across encoder architectures; encoder pretraining determines the difficulty threshold at which adaptation becomes critical. Systematic ablation across two encoders (DeBERTa-v3, RoBERTa) and three OOD attack families (DD, FITD, ActorAttack) reveals:\\n\\n**On Deceptive Delight (moderate OOD)**: DeBERTa vanilla collapses (F1=0.31±0.03), continued pretraining bridges ~60pp (MLM=0.91±0.12), classification achieves 0.997±0.005. RoBERTa vanilla already succeeds (0.981±0.026) — hierarchy is flat. Scrambled=binary=9-class (all 0.997±0.005).\\n\\n**On ActorAttack (hard OOD)**: RoBERTa vanilla ALSO collapses (0.466±0.163), proving \\\"no DA needed\\\" was DD-specific. DeBERTa vanilla=0.258±0.000. Both encoders benefit from DA: DeBERTa 9-class=0.972±0.026, RoBERTa 9-class=0.939±0.046. Binary (0.990±0.008) ≈ 9-class (0.972±0.026) ≈ scrambled (0.951±0.043) — all three classification variants equivalent at Full (max gap 3.9pp, variance intervals fully overlap, n=3). Label quality is not a significant factor on ActorAttack, consistent with DD findings (D032).\\n\\n**On Human-Written Jailbreaks (exp8, ToxicChat, macro F1 — D036)**: Domain-adapted variants outperform vanilla on human-authored data from ToxicChat (LMSYS, 204 jailbreaks + 204 benign, balanced, no data leakage). Macro F1: JB-MLM=0.837±0.013, wiki-MLM=0.791±0.093, binary=0.705±0.072, scrambled=0.616±0.056, 9-class=0.596±0.087, topic=0.496±0.042, vanilla=0.413±0.021. All DA variants exceed vanilla (+18-42pp), confirming features learned from Qwen3-8B synthetic data transfer to human text. JB-MLM strongest on human data (reversed from synthetic where 9-class leads), suggesting pure domain exposure transfers better across the human/LLM style gap. Notably, wiki-MLM (0.791, out-of-domain text) outperforms ALL classification variants (binary=0.705, scrambled=0.616, 9-class=0.596), further demonstrating that MLM domain exposure mechanism dominates classification supervision on human data. The jb_mlm vs wiki_mlm gap (4.6pp, std overlap) is modest, suggesting domain specificity provides marginal benefit on human data. 9-class drops from best on synthetic OOD (0.997/0.972) to 5th on human data (0.596), indicating classification supervision overfits to synthetic data distribution. Paper must discuss this contrast explicitly. NOTE: verazuo/jailbreak_llms results INVALID (benign data leakage, M014) — removed from paper.\\n\\n**Cross-encoder CKA (D029)**: Both MLM and 9-class reshape representations substantially (centered CKA < 0.5 vs vanilla), but representation change magnitude ≠ detection improvement. RoBERTa MLM changes more (CKA=0.393) yet gains only 0.6pp; DeBERTa MLM changes less (CKA=0.442) yet gains 60pp. The starting point determines the return on adaptation.\\n\\n**Cross-LLM Generalization (D049/D051, exp16/exp16b/exp17)**: On mixed Qwen+Llama training data (exp16, 250+250, 3 seeds), the evidence hierarchy REVERSES to 9-class >> MLM ≈ vanilla. DD OOD: 9-class=0.818±0.096, MLM=0.289±0.101, vanilla=0.291±0.065. ToxicChat human data (D051 correction, balanced 408-sample eval): 9-class=0.550±0.052, vanilla=0.356±0.009 — NO improvement over Qwen-only (exp8 9-class=0.596±0.087, stds overlap). Mixed training eliminates single-LLM confound but does NOT improve human-data generalization. On Llama-only training (exp16b, seed 42): MLM ≤ vanilla on all OOD (Llama DD: MLM=0.191, vanilla=0.181; Llama AA: MLM=0.290, vanilla=0.411 — vanilla better by 12pp). Scaling to 1000 Qwen conversations (exp17, 3 seeds): 9-class maintains dominance on OOD (DD=0.889±0.041, AA=0.913±0.041) while MLM remains weak (DD=0.338±0.010, AA=0.351±0.018, ≈ vanilla). **Critical revision**: MLM effectiveness is specific to single-source (Qwen) training data; classification-based DAPT (9-class) is the ONLY robust adaptation across all training data compositions.\\n\\n**Key nuances**: (1) Binary ≈ 9-class ≈ scrambled on ActorAttack Full (0.990 vs 0.972 vs 0.951, all within noise at n=3; D032). Label quality is not a detection-relevant factor on any OOD difficulty. (2) Classification supervision consistently reduces cross-seed variance 4-20x (Claim 3): 9-class std=0.005-0.026 vs MLM std=0.080-0.115, across both DD and ActorAttack. (3) Perturbation robustness is attack-family-dependent (Discussion finding): DD OOD Δ≈0, ActorAttack DA variants Δ=-0.17 to -0.35 (vanilla -0.009 floor effect). DA detection advantage survives perturbation (McNemar p<0.001) as Claim 1 corollary. (4) **D049**: MLM's ~60pp improvement on DD OOD is Qwen-specific; on mixed-LLM and Llama-only data, MLM ≈ vanilla. 9-class is the only universally robust DAPT variant.\\n\\n## Novelty Positioning (D030 reframe)\\nNOT: \\\"Persuasion-aware fine-tuning is the critical ingredient\\\" (MLM control falsified this on DD).\\nNOT: \\\"RoBERTa doesn't need domain adaptation\\\" (ActorAttack falsified this — D030).\\nNOT: \\\"Label quality matters on hard attacks\\\" (3-seed ActorAttack: binary 0.990 ≈ 9-class 0.972 ≈ scrambled 0.951, all within noise; D032).\\nNOT: \\\"Perturbation robustness generalizes across attack families\\\" (exp6 falsified: DD Δ≈0 but ActorAttack Δ=-0.17 to -0.35 for all variants).\\nNOT: \\\"MLM domain exposure is the universal mechanism\\\" (D049: MLM effectiveness is Qwen-specific; on mixed-LLM and Llama-only data, MLM ≈ vanilla).\\nYES: \\\"We systematically decompose cross-attack generalization across two encoder architectures, three OOD attack families, and two training data compositions. The key finding: classification-based domain adaptation (9-class) is the only universally robust adaptation variant — it improves generalization regardless of encoder architecture, attack complexity, or training data source. MLM continued pretraining is effective on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training, revealing its effectiveness is condition-dependent. Classification supervision provides additional benefits: cross-seed stability (std reduced 4-20x vs MLM on synthetic OOD) and interpretability via strategy trajectories. Mixed-LLM training eliminates the single-LLM confound without degrading performance (exp16 ToxicChat=0.550±0.052 ≈ exp8 0.596±0.087 on balanced eval). DA's detection advantage survives adversarial perturbation (McNemar p<0.001), though absolute perturbation robustness is attack-family-dependent. Label quality (correct vs scrambled vs binary) does not significantly affect detection at any OOD difficulty level (D032).\\\"\\n\\n## Inherent Lexical Separability (D009)\\nPersuasive jailbreak conversations and general benign conversations are inherently lexically separable (TF-IDF≈1.0 on ALL data sources including real WildChat). TF-IDF remains strong on DD OOD (Full=0.961, per-prefix K1-K5 near-perfect). **But TF-IDF collapses on ActorAttack (Full=0.261, exp7)** — lexical features cannot capture multi-persona role-playing attack structure. DisPeD's value: (1) bridging 71pp gap vs TF-IDF on hard OOD (ActorAttack: 9-class=0.972 vs TF-IDF=0.261), (2) cross-seed stability (std 4-20x smaller than MLM variants), (3) interpretability via strategy trajectories.\\n\\n## Comprehensive Ablation Picture (D014, updated D019/D020, revised D049)\\nSystematic ablation reveals the method's actual mechanism:\\n- **Critical (conditioned, D049)**: On single-source Qwen data, domain exposure in any form (MLM, scrambled, persuasion) bridges OOD gap from F1=0.28 to F1≈1.0. **However**, on mixed-LLM (Qwen+Llama) and different-source (Llama-only) data, only classification-based DAPT (9-class) reliably bridges the gap; MLM effectiveness is condition-dependent (Qwen-specific, provides \\n\\n... Mixed-LLM training eliminates the single-LLM confound without degrading performance (exp16 ToxicChat=0.550±0.052 ≈ exp8 0.596±0.087 on balanced eval). DA's detection advantage survives adversarial perturbation (McNemar p<0.001), though absolute perturbation robustness is attack-family-dependent. Label quality (correct vs scrambled vs binary) does not significantly affect detection at any OOD difficulty level (D032).\\\"\\n\\n## Inherent Lexical Separability (D009)\\nPersuasive jailbreak conversations and general benign conversations are inherently lexically separable (TF-IDF≈1.0 on ALL data sources including real WildChat). TF-IDF remains strong on DD OOD (Full=0.961, per-prefix K1-K5 near-perfect). **But TF-IDF collapses on ActorAttack (Full=0.261, exp7)** — lexical features cannot capture multi-persona role-playing attack structure. DisPeD's value: (1) bridging 71pp gap vs TF-IDF on hard OOD (ActorAttack: 9-class=0.972 vs TF-IDF=0.261), (2) cross-seed stability (std 4-20x smaller than MLM variants), (3) interpretability via strategy trajectories.\\n\\n## Comprehensive Ablation Picture (D014, updated D019/D020, revised D049)\\nSystematic ablation reveals the method's actual mechanism:\\n- **Critical (conditioned, D049)**: On single-source Qwen data, domain exposure in any form (MLM, scrambled, persuasion) bridges OOD gap from F1=0.28 to F1≈1.0. **However**, on mixed-LLM (Qwen+Llama) and different-source (Llama-only) data, only classification-based DAPT (9-class) reliably bridges the gap; MLM effectiveness is condition-dependent (Qwen-specific, provides \\n\\n... - 131 experiments total, 98 with conclusions (70 success, 27 negative, 0 failed)
- R17 Overall 3.0 (Findings level)
- Key concerns from R17: ToxicChat reversal, IID benign scope, Topic main text, Novelty
- DDL May 25 (~3 days)
- GPU idle (cuda:0, ~98GB)
- R18 already submitted


- I've already built out solid coverage across the core evidence hierarchy with multiple encoder variants, validated the approach across different architectures, tested mixed-LLM training scenarios, and established statistical rigor through bootstrap and TOST procedures with multiple seeds. The benign adaptation work with WildChat and OASST2 is solid, perturbation robustness checks are in place, and I've diagnosed the topic failure modes and turn count baselines.

- Looking at what's left to address, the ToxicChat reversal finding is well-grounded in the FP/FN decomposition and truncation analysis—it's fundamentally a gap between human and synthetic data that's hard to close experimentally. The novelty concern appears to be cut off, but I should see what else needs examining. F4)**: This is structural, not solvable by experiments. The contribution is "systematic decomposition" + "discriminative compression mechanism". More experiments won't help novelty perception.

3. **IID benign scope (MF2)**: Already addressed with WildChat + OASST2 benign adaptation experiments. The abstract now states "(attack-side OOD, benign IID)".

4. **Topic vs "any partition" (MF3)**: Already addressed with exp61 (random partitions) + exp62 (topic failure diagnosis).

Looking at what supplementary experiments might still be worth pursuing: testing a third benign source beyond WildChat and OASST2 would strengthen the adaptation story, but finding and processing a new dataset is probably too time-intensive. Fine-grained ToxicChat analysis is limited since it's single-turn with no sub-categories. The more promising angle is validating RoBERTa-large on ToxicChat to see if the DAPT utility is scale-dependent—if the larger model works well without DAPT, that would support the claim more broadly, though it requires having the checkpoints available and time for training and evaluation.

I could also analyze prediction confidence from the existing 9-class vs MLM results to understand whether false positives are high-confidence or borderline, which would strengthen the discussion of why 9-class has such high FPR and could be extracted from the raw predictions in about 30 minutes. Threshold sensitivity analysis is another option—finding the optimal threshold for 9-class on ToxicChat could show whether the FP issue is calibration-related, though that risks post-hoc tuning on test data. The cross-seed variance analysis using the 3-seed data is partially covered already.

Looking at other potential experiments: the mixed-LLM ToxicChat validation has already been done with the 0.550±0.052 result mentioned in the paper, and additional TOST with 10 seeds on ActorAttack is complete. There's an interesting idea about benign adaptation on ToxicChat—using a few ToxicChat benign examples to see if FPR drops, similar to the WildChat/OASST2 adaptation work—but there's a fundamental issue: ToxicChat is single-turn while the model is designed for multi-turn conversations.

The WildChat and OASST2 adaptation experiments already showed strong results (FPR→0% and FPR→1.0% respectively with small benign samples), so the question is whether we can replicate this on ToxicChat. The challenge is that ToxicChat serves as the test set, so using ToxicChat benign for adaptation would create data leakage unless we carefully separate the benign examples used for training from those used for evaluation. With 204 benign examples in ToxicChat, we could use a subset like N=10 for adaptation and evaluate on the remaining 194 benign examples plus the toxic test set.

This would directly address the reviewer's main concern about ToxicChat performance by demonstrating that the two-step protocol (DAPT plus benign adaptation) works on real human data when properly implemented. The current paper shows high FPR on ToxicChat precisely because it only uses synthetic benign data during training, so adding this adaptation experiment would validate that the mechanism generalizes. That said, the paper already frames this as a benign-side shift problem and shows adaptation works on other datasets, so adding ToxicChat would be a third validation point but requires careful handling to avoid data leakage.

Now considering whether to test a scaled-down DeBERTa variant to explore model efficiency trade-offs.

For the ActorAttack analysis, I could examine which specific samples the scrambled model misclassifies compared to the 9-class model to understand what label semantics matter most on complex attacks, though this would be qualitative rather than requiring GPU resources.

Looking at the most promising options, the ToxicChat benign adaptation experiment stands out as the highest-impact choice—taking a small set of benign examples from ToxicChat, retraining the second stage, and evaluating on held-out test data would directly show whether FPR improves and whether this addresses the main reviewer feedback.

However, there's a critical mismatch I'm noticing: WildChat and OASST2 are multi-turn conversations, but ToxicChat is single-turn, so adding single-turn benign examples would work differently than the paper's original adaptation mechanism—the model would see single-turn inputs through a pipeline designed for multi-turn data, which changes the dynamics of how the BiGRU processes these examples.

The core problem is that the 9-class model already has a low macro F1 (0.596) due to high false positive rates (51%), which the paper frames as condition-specific, so testing adaptation on ToxicChat benign data would be investigating something different from what the paper actually claims to address.

Option 2 would be analyzing the confidence distribution of false positives from the existing experiments—checking whether they're high-confidence predictions (suggesting the model genuinely confuses benign text with jailbreaks) or borderline (suggesting threshold tuning could help). This adds mechanistic insight to the discussion and anticipates reviewer questions, with no risk since it's just analyzing existing predictions.

Option 3 would correlate the false positives with text properties like length, topic, and vocabulary overlap with the synthetic training data to see if errors cluster on specific text types. Both of these are CPU-only analyses that don't require GPU time, which shifts the focus away from what was originally being asked about.

Looking at what's actually feasible with GPU time, the options are limited—either adapting ToxicChat (which has the problems I identified), running additional seeds with diminishing returns, or testing on a different jailbreak dataset that would need to be found and processed first.

The real insight here is that the experimental work is already comprehensive: 131 experiments across multiple configurations, architectures, training approaches, attack families, and statistical tests. The remaining concerns about ToxicChat reversal and novelty aren't things more experiments can fix—they're structural issues about how the model overfits to the synthetic distribution and how the findings are positioned in the literature. The paper honestly frames these as condition-specific results with clear limitations, so additional experiments won't resolve those fundamental questions.

There is one non-GPU analysis worth doing: diving into the confidence and error patterns on ToxicChat false positives using the existing experiment data, which could strengthen the Discussion section's explanation of why the 9-class false positive rate is elevated. But that's analysis work, not a new experiment requiring GPU resources.

WildChat high FPR is genuine domain shift, not calibration artifact.\"}, \"conclusion\": \"negative\"}, \"exp37_length_matched_benign\": {\"hypothesis\": \"9-class 模型不依赖 turn count 作为检测 shortcut：截断 benign 对话到 jailbreak 匹配长度 (3-10 turns) 后 FPR 不会显著上升，WildChat 短对话 (3-10 turns) 的 FPR 也不高于长对话，因为 DAPT 训练迫使模型学习说服策略内容特征而非 length 统计量。\", \"metrics\": {\"part1_wildchat_fpr\": {\"short_3_10\": {\"fpr_mean\": 0.77, \"fpr_std\": 0.105, \"n\": 196}, \"medium_11_15\": {\"fpr_mean\": 0.942, \"fpr_std\": 0.054, \"n\": 23}, \"long_16_plus\": {\"fpr_mean\": 0.857, \"fpr_std\": 0.117, \"n\": 7}, \"note\": \"Short bucket FPR LOWER than Medium — length is NOT the driver of WildChat FPR\"}, \"part2_benign_truncation\": {\"original_fpr\": 0, \"K3_fpr\": 0, \"K5_fpr\": 0, \"K8_fpr\": 0, \"K10_fpr\": 0, \"note\": \"FPR=0% at ALL truncation levels including K=3. Model does not use length as shortcut.\"}, \"part3_jailbreak_padding\": {\"DD_original\": 0.996, \"DD_padded\": 0.971, \"DD_delta\": -0.025, \"AA_original\": 0.963, \"AA_padded\": 0.383, \"AA_delta\": -0.579, \"note\": \"DD resilient to padding. AA collapses (-57.9pp). BiGRU attention dilution on subtle attacks.\"}}, \"conclusion\": \"negative\"}, \"exp36\": {\"hypothesis\": \"MLM DAPT encoder produces weaker frozen representations than classification (9-class) DAPT encoder for OOD jailbreak detection, because classification training forces the encoder to learn task-relevant discriminative features while MLM only learns general language patterns.\", \"metrics\": {\"iid_full\": {\"mean\": 0.982, \"std\": 0.016}, \"dd_ood_full\": {\"mean\": 0.993, \"std\": 0.009}, \"aa_ood_full\": {\"mean\": 0.941, \"std\": 0.037}, \"fitd_ood_full\": {\"mean\": 0.987, \"std\": 0.009}, \"dd_ood_k1\": {\"mean\": 0.953, \"std\": 0.027}, \"dd_ood_k2\": {\"mean\": 0.977, \"std\": 0.021}, \"dd_ood_k3\": {\"mean\": 0.987, \"std\": 0.005}, \"dd_ood_k5\": {\"mean\": 0.997, \"std\": 0.005}}, \"conclusion\": \"negative\"}, \"exp31_lambda_ablation_aa\": {\"hypothesis\": \"Multi-task persuasion loss weight (lambda) affects OOD generalization in E2E fine-tuning: lambda=0 (intent-only) will underperform on hard OOD (ActorAttack) compared to lambda>0, because the persuasion auxiliary signal provides a regularization effect that preserves OOD-relevant features in the encoder. There exists an optimal lambda that maximizes AA OOD without sacrificing IID performance.\", \"metrics\": {\"lambda_0_aa\": 1, \"lambda_0.1_aa\": 0.9904, \"lambda_0.5_aa\": 0.9809, \"lambda_1.0_aa\": 0.9809, \"lambda_2.0_aa\": 0.9439, \"iid_all\": 1, \"dd_all\": 1, \"fitd_all\": 1, \"note\": \"Higher lambda monotonically hurts AA OOD. lambda=0 (intent-only E2E) achieves perfect AA. Persuasion aux loss acts as a distractor during E2E.\"}, \"conclusion\": \"negative\"}, \"exp27_mean_pooling\": {\"hypothesis\": \"Mean pooling (no temporal modeling) with end-to-end fine-tuning will match BiGRU E2E on easy OOD (DD) but degrade on hard OOD (ActorAttack), demonstrating that temporal modeling via BiGRU is necessary for detecting multi-turn adaptive attacks.\", \"metrics\": {\"iid_full\": {\"mean\": 1, \"std\": 0}, \"dd_ood_k1\": {\"mean\": 1, \"std\": 0}, \"dd_ood_k2\": {\"mean\": 1, \"std\": 0}, \"dd_ood_k3\": {\"mean\": 1, \"std\": 0}, \"dd_ood_k5\": {\"mean\": 1, \"std\": 0}, \"dd_ood_full\": {\"mean\": 1, \"std\": 0}, \"aa_ood_full\": {\"mean\": 1, \"std\": 0}, \"fitd_ood_full\": {\"mean\": 1, \"std\": 0}, \"metric\": \"f1_macro\", \"seeds\": 3, \"early_stop_epoch\": 4}, \"conclusion\": \"negative\"}, \"exp19_wildchat_fpr\": {\"hypothesis\": \"9-class DAPT classifier maintains FPR < 15% on high-drift real-world benign conversations from WildChat (after excluding jailbreak-contaminated samples), demonstrating external validity beyond synthetic benign data and addressing reviewer concern about deployment false positive rates\", \"conclusion\": \"negative\", \"metrics\": {\"dataset\": \"wildchat_benign_226 (226 filtered multi-turn benign, avg 7.4 turns)\", \"9class_fpr\": {\"seed42\": 0.668, \"seed123\": 0.611, \"seed456\": 0.765, \"mean\": 0.681, \"std\": 0.064}, \"mlm_fpr\": {\"seed42\": 0.367, \"seed123\": 0.646, \"seed456\": 0.323, \"mean\": 0.445, \"std\": 0.143}, \"vanilla_fpr\": {\"seed42\": 0.473, \"seed123\": 0.425, \"seed456\": 0.544, \"mean\": 0.481, \"std\": 0.049}, \"note\": \"NEGATIVE: All variants show extreme FPR on real-world benign. 9-class worst (68.1%). Used exp17 checkpoints (Qwen 1000 scale). Severe distribution shift between synthetic training benign and real WildChat conversations.\"}}, \"exp15c_roberta_large_mlm\": {\"hypothesis\": \"MLM-only continued pretraining on RoBERTa-large (without classification head) will NOT cause the same performance regression as 9-class DAPT, because the regression stems from classification fine-tuning conflicting with frozen-encoder GRU evaluation, not from domain adaptation itself.\", \"metrics\": {\"dd_ood\": {\"mlm\": {\"k1\": 0.876, \"k3\": 0.902, \"full\": 0.884}, \"vanilla_exp15\": {\"k1\": 0.878, \"k3\": 0.915, \"full\": 0.927}, \"9class_exp15\": {\"k1\": 0.965, \"k3\": 0.96, \"full\": 0.987}}, \"actorattack\": {\"mlm\": {\"k1\": 0.876, \"k3\": 0.881, \"full\": 0.874}, \"vanilla_exp15\": {\"k1\": 0.977, \"k3\": 0.99, \"full\": 1}, \"9class_exp15\": {\"k1\": 0.718, \"k3\": 0.881, \"full\": 0.946}}, \"key_finding\": \"MLM DAPT hurts both DD OOD and AA vs vanilla. AA: MLM 0.874 < 9class 0.946 < vanilla 1.000. Representation shift from continued pretraining itself conflicts with frozen-encoder GRU, not classification head interference.\"}, \"conclusion\": \"negative\"}, \"exp15b_roberta_large_lr\": {\"hypothesis\": \"exp15 9class underperformance on AA (0.946 vs vanilla 1.000) is caused by catastrophic forgetting from too-high LR (2e-5) for 355M model. Reducing DAPT LR to 1e-5 should preserve pretrained representations while still learning persuasion patterns, recovering AA F1 to match or exceed vanilla.\", \"conclusion\": \"negative\", \"metrics\": {\"dd_ood\": {\"9class_lr1e5\": {\"k1\": 0.899, \"k3\": 0.851, \"full\": 0.948}, \"9class_lr2e5_exp15\": {\"k1\": 0.965, \"k3\": 0.96, \"full\": 0.987}}, \"actorattack\": {\"9class_lr1e5\": {\"k1\": 0.459, \"k3\": 0.676, \"full\": 0.781}, \"9class_lr2e5_exp15\": {\"k1\": 0.718, \"k3\": 0.881, \"full\": 0.946}}, \"key_finding\": \"LR=1e-5 worse than LR=2e-5 on both DD and AA. Catastrophic forgetting hypothesis falsified. The capacity-DAPT interaction on AA is real, not a hyperparameter artifact.\"}}, \"exp15_roberta_large\": {\"hypothesis\": \"RoBERTa-large (355M) vanilla will still underperform DAPT variants on ActorAttack OOD, demonstrating that DAPT value is independent of model capacity. If RoBERTa-large vanilla matches DAPT performance, then the difficulty threshold is a capacity effect rather than a representation quality effect.\", \"conclusion\": \"negative\", \"metrics\": {\"dd_ood\": {\"roberta_large_vanilla\": {\"k1\": 0.878, \"k3\": 0.915, \"full\": 0.927}, \"roberta_large_9class\": {\"k1\": 0.965, \"k3\": 0.96, \"full\": 0.987}}, \"actorattack\": {\"roberta_large_vanilla\": {\"k1\": 0.977, \"k3\": 0.99, \"full\": 1}, \"roberta_large_9class\": {\"k1\": 0.718, \"k3\": 0.881, \"full\": 0.946}}, \"key_finding\": \"Hypothesis falsified on AA: RoBERTa-large vanilla (1.000) > 9class (0.946). Capacity effect dominates on AA. DD OOD: DAPT still helps (0.987 vs 0.927).\"}}, \"exp14_cross_llm_validation\": {\"hypothesis\": \"DAPT advantage (9-class/jb_mlm >> vanilla) is not specific to Qwen3-8B-generated data.       "result": "{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"experiments\": {\"exp63_toxicchat_fpfn\": {\"hypothesis\": \"9-class ToxicChat performance gap vs MLM is dominated by false positives (>70%), because classification DAPT over-encodes synthetic distributional features that trigger on human benign text\", \"conclusion\": \"negative\", \"metrics\": {\"9class_mv_f1\": 0.652, \"9class_mv_fpr\": 0.51, \"9class_mv_precision\": 0.589, \"9class_mv_recall\": 0.73, \"mlm_mv_f1\": 0.853, \"mlm_mv_fpr\": 0.167, \"mlm_mv_precision\": 0.839, \"mlm_mv_recall\": 0.868, \"mlm_correct_9class_wrong_total\": 144, \"mlm_wins_fp_fraction\": 0.646, \"mlm_wins_fn_fraction\": 0.354, \"9class_correct_mlm_wrong_total\": 46}}, \"exp62_topic_diagnosis\": {\"hypothesis\": \"Topic DAPT fails because it blurs the jailbreak/benign boundary in embedding space — topic classification teaches features orthogonal to the jailbreak detection task, resulting in lower Fisher separability between jailbreak and benign representations\", \"conclusion\": \"negative\", \"metrics\": {\"vanilla_iid_fisher\": 0.118, \"vanilla_dd_fisher\": 0.401, \"vanilla_aa_fisher\": 0.408, \"9class_iid_fisher\": 0.073, \"9class_dd_fisher\": 0.2, \"9class_aa_fisher\": 0.832, \"topic_iid_fisher\": 0.108, \"topic_dd_fisher\": 0.653, \"topic_aa_fisher\": 0.311, \"finding\": \"9-class DAPT has LOWER Fisher/silhouette than vanilla on IID/DD, not higher — mechanism is richer geometry not simple separability. Topic DAPT failure is not boundary-blurring.\"}}, \"exp55_5seed_mlm_vs_9class_aa\": {\"hypothesis\": \"9-class persuasion-grounded DeBERTa DAPT produces significantly better OOD generalization than domain-exposure-only MLM DAPT, with the advantage being robust across 5 random seeds (expanding from 3 to address reviewer W2 concern about insufficient seed count)\", \"metrics\": {\"n_seeds\": 5, \"seeds\": [42, 123, 456, 789, 1024], \"9class_aa_ood\": {\"mean\": 0.9735, \"std\": 0.0124}, \"mlm_aa_ood\": {\"mean\": 0.9566, \"std\": 0.0237}, \"9class_iid\": {\"mean\": 1, \"std\": 0}, \"aa_ood_mlm_wins\": 1, \"total_time_min\": 10.5}, \"conclusion\": \"negative\"}, \"exp56_crossllm_10shot_adaptation\": {\"hypothesis\": \"10-shot benign adaptation with Llama-origin conversations \"aa_f1\": 0.271, \"fitd_f1\": 0.99, \"dd_llama_recall\": 0.08, \"aa_llama_recall\": 0, \"fitd_llama_recall\": 0.52}}}, \"conclusion\": \"negative\"}, \"exp48_ensemble_mlm_9class\": {\"hypothesis\": \"Ensembling JB-MLM (strongest on ToxicChat F1=0.837) and 9-class (strongest on synthetic OOD) will outperform either individual model across all benchmarks, because their error patterns are complementary: MLM captures general linguistic anomaly while 9-class captures persuasion-strategy-specific features.\", \"conclusion\": \"negative\", \"metrics\": {\"jb_mlm_baseline\": {\"tc_f1\": 0.82, \"wc_fpr\": 0.306, \"dd_f1\": 1, \"aa_f1\": 0.99}, \"9class_baseline\": {\"tc_f1\": 0.596, \"wc_fpr\": 0.755, \"dd_f1\": 1, \"aa_f1\": 0.99}, \"best_vote_and\": {\"tc_f1\": 0.735, \"wc_fpr\": representations encode transferable persuasion signals that generalize to real human multi-turn jailbreak attempts (MHJ dataset), achieving higher detection recall than vanilla DeBERTa because human attackers use similar persuasion strategies as synthetic attacks.\", \"conclusion\": \"negative\", \"metrics\": {\"mhj_status\": \"gated_access_required\", \"fallback_dataset\": \"verazuo/jailbreak_llms (exp8)\", \"verazuo_9class_f1\": 0.812, \"verazuo_9class_f1_std\": 0.135, \"verazuo_vanilla_f1\": 0.131, \"verazuo_mlm_f1\": 0.968, \"verazuo_9class_vs_vanilla_pp\": 68, \"verazuo_n_jailbreak\": 666, \"verazuo_n_benign\": 213, \"note\": \"MHJ multi-turn dataset requires HuggingFace gated access application. Existing verazuo single-turn human jailbreak data (exp8) confirms +68pp transfer. No new training performed.\"}}, \"exp41_tost_10seed\": {\"hypothesis\": \"Extending TOST equivalence test from n=5 to n=10 seeds will resolve the inconclusive AA result (p=0.08 at n=5) by providing sufficient statistical power: if scrambled and 9-class are truly equivalent within 5pp margin on ActorAttack, the increased sample size will yield p<0.05, confirming label content irrelevance across all OOD families.\", \"metrics\": {\"n_seeds\": 10, \"seeds\": [42, 123, 456, 789, 1000, 2024, 2025, 2026, 314, 628], \"tost_DD\": {\"p\": 2.06e-09, \"equivalent\": true, \"cohens_d\": -0.136}, \"tost_AA\": {\"p\": 0.183, \"equivalent\": false, \"cohens_d\": 0.44}, \"tost_FITD\": {\"p\": 0, \"equivalent\": true, \"cohens_d\": 0}, \"9class_AA_mean\": 0.979, \"scrambled_AA_mean\": 0.949, \"AA_mean_diff_pp\": 3, \"training_time_min\": 16.8}, \"conclusion\": \"negative\"}, \"exp38_wildcat_calibration\": {\"hypothesis\": \"少量 WildChat benign 样本 (~50 条) 即可通过阈值校准将 FPR 降至 <25% 且 TPR >90%，证明 domain calibration 是可行的 practical path，WildChat 高 FPR 是阈值问题而非表征能力问题。\", \"metrics\": {\"roc_auc\": {\"mean\": 0.7, \"std\": 0.054, \"per_seed\": {\"seed42\": 0.768, \"seed123\": 0.697, \"seed456\": 0.635}}, \"default_t05_fpr\": {\"mean\": 0.791, \"std\": 0.098}, \"threshold_calibration_held_out\": {\"tpr95_fpr\": {\"mean\": 0.627, \"std\": 0.1}, \"tpr90_fpr\": {\"mean\": 0.587, \"std\": 0.091}}, \"temperature_scaling\": {\"learned_T\": \"20-40 (severe overconfidence)\", \"temp_scaled_tpr95_fpr\": 0.627, \"temp_scaled_tpr90_fpr\": 0.583, \"note\": \"Temperature scaling does NOT change FPR because ROC is invariant to monotonic score transforms\"}, \"note\": \"AUC=0.70 is the fundamental ceiling. Threshold calibration drops FPR from 79%→63% (at TPR95), still far from <25% target. WildChat high FPR is genuine domain shift, not calibration artifact.\"}, \"conclusion\": \"negative\"}, \"exp37_length_matched_benign\": {\"hypothesis\": \"9-class 模型不依赖 turn count 作为检测 shortcut：截断 benign 对话到 jailbreak 匹配长度 (3-10 turns) 后 FPR 不会显著上升，WildChat 短对话 (3-10 turns) 的 FPR 也不高于长对话，因为 DAPT 训练迫使模型学习说服策略内容特征而非 length 统计量。\", \"metrics\": {\"part1_wildchat_fpr\": {\"short_3_10\": {\"fpr_mean\": 0.77, \"fpr_std\": 0.105, \"n\": 196}, \"medium_11_15\": {\"fpr_mean\": 0.942, \"fpr_std\": 0.054, \"n\": 23}, \"long_16_plus\": {\"fpr_mean\": 0.857, \"fpr_std\": 0.117, \"n\": 7}, \"note\": \"Short bucket FPR LOWER than Medium — length is NOT the driver of WildChat FPR\"}, \"part2_benign_truncation\": {\"original_fpr\": 0, \"K3_fpr\": 0, \"K5_fpr\": 0, \"K8_fpr\": 0, \"K10_fpr\": 0, \"note\": \"FPR=0% at ALL truncation levels including K=3. Model does not use length as shortcut.\"}, \"part3_jailbreak_padding\": {\"DD_original\": 0.996, \"DD_padded\": 0.971, \"DD_delta\": -0.025, \"AA_original\": 0.963, \"AA_padded\": 0.383, \"AA_delta\": -0.579, \"note\": \"DD resilient to padding. AA collapses (-57.9pp). BiGRU attention dilution on subtle attacks.\"}}, \"conclusion\": \"negative\"}, \"exp36\": {\"hypothesis\": \"MLM DAPT encoder produces weaker frozen representations than classification (9-class) DAPT encoder for OOD jailbreak detection, because classification training forces the encoder to learn task-relevant discriminative features while MLM only learns general language patterns.\", \"metrics\": {\"iid_full\": {\"mean\": 0.982, \"std\": 0.016}, \"dd_ood_full\": {\"mean\": 0.993, \"std\": 0.009}, \"aa_ood_full\": {\"mean\": 0.941, \"std\": 0.037}, \"fitd_ood_full\": {\"mean\": 0.987, \"std\": 0.009}, \"dd_ood_k1\": {\"mean\": 0.953, \"std\": 0.027}, \"dd_ood_k2\": {\"mean\": 0.977, \"std\": 0.021}, \"dd_ood_k3\": {\"mean\": 0.987, \"std\": 0.005}, \"dd_ood_k5\": {\"mean\": 0.997, \"std\": 0.005}}, \"conclusion\": \"negative\"}, \"exp31_lambda_ablation_aa\": {\"hypothesis\": \"Multi-task persuasion loss weight (lambda) affects OOD generalization in E2E fine-tuning: lambda=0 (intent-only) will underperform on hard OOD (ActorAttack) compared to lambda>0, because the persuasion auxiliary signal provides a regularization effect that preserves OOD-relevant features in the encoder. <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have a comprehensive view of the 28 negative experiments and the overall experiment landscape. Let me now systematically assess potential supplementary experiments.

**Analysis of gaps:**

1. **ToxicChat (MF1)**: Thoroughly covered by exp8, exp12 (bootstrap), exp58 (truncation), exp63 (FP/FN). The reversal is a fundamental finding: classification DAPT overfits to synthetic distribution, MLM domain exposure transfers better. This is properly discussed as "condition-specific advantage."

2. **Novelty (MF4)**: Cannot be addressed by experiments. It's about positioning - "systematic decomposition" + "discriminative compression mechanism" + "condition-dependent effectiveness map." The 13-configuration ablation IS the novelty.
   
3. **Any partition vs Topic (MF3)**: Covered by exp61 (10 random binary partitions, 9/10 work on AA) and exp62 (topic failure = temporal inconsistency). Well-addressed.

4. **IID benign (MF2)**: Acknowledged explicitly in abstract/intro/experiments. WildChat + OASST2 adaptation shown.

Looking at potential high-value experiments, I could analyze prediction probabilities from the ToxicChat classification results to see whether the false positives are high-confidence mistakes or borderline cases—this would strengthen the mechanistic explanation in the Discussion section.

The larger-scale statistical power analysis is already complete at n=10, and I've covered four distinct attack families across the experiments. Testing additional baselines like RoBERTa variants on ToxicChat could be informative, though the current test set sizes (76-118 per family) make leave-one-out validation impractical.

Looking at what I've already done, the 131 experiments span 13 encoder configurations across 3 architectures tested against 4 attack families, plus mixed-LLM training, human validation on ToxicChat, and statistical testing with bootstrap and permutation methods. The experimental coverage feels solid.

The next chunk covers representation analysis techniques like SV, CKA, Fisher, and probing, along with benign adaptation curves, perturbation robustness, turn count baselines, inference efficiency, LLM-as-judge comparisons, topic failure diagnosis, random partition validation, data efficiency analysis, and FP/FN decomposition. These all feed into the core narrative.

The fundamental limitations—ToxicChat reversal and limited novelty—stem from the study design itself using synthetic training data, so no additional experiments will resolve them. A confidence analysis on ToxicChat predictions from existing data could be useful but adds minimal value at this point, and the risk of last-minute integration isn't worth it. The current evidence base is sufficient for the paper's claims, so there's no high-ROI GPU work left to pursue.
```

{
  "text": "## 补充实验评估 — DDL 前 GPU 利用\n\n**结论**: 当前数据已充分，无高收益 GPU 实验可补。\n\n---\n\n### 评估方法\n\n逐项审查 R17 四个 critical blockers + 论文 3 个 core claims，对照 131 实验覆盖度，筛选 <2h GPU + 可整合的候选实验。\n\n### 已充分覆盖的维度\n\n| 维度 | 覆盖实验 | 状态 |\n|------|---------|------|\n| ToxicChat 反转机制 | exp8 + exp12(bootstrap) + exp58(truncation) + exp63(FP/FN) | ✅ 完整 |\n| Topic 失败原因 | exp62(Fisher/temporal) + exp61(random partition) | ✅ 完整 |\n| IID benign 声明 | exp19(WildChat) + exp51(OASST2) + exp45(adaptation curve) | ✅ 完整 |\n| 统计严谨性 | exp41(TOST n=10) + exp12(bootstrap) + exp55(5-seed AA) | ✅ 完整 |\n| Discriminative compression | exp49(representation) + exp50(layerwise) + exp54(causal probe) + exp57(SVD IID-only) | ✅ 完整 |\n| Cross-architecture | exp1(RoBERTa-base) + exp15(RoBERTa-large) + BERT proxy | ✅ 完整 |\n| Mixed-LLM | exp16/16b/17 + exp44 | ✅ 完整 |\n| Perturbation | exp6(AA) + plan_003/013/016v2/017(DD) | ✅ 完整 |\n| Temporal modeling | exp13(mean pool) + exp27 + shuffle ablation | ✅ 完整 |\n\n### 候选实验逐项评估\n\n**A. ToxicChat benign adaptation（~1.5h GPU）**\n- 用 N=10-25 ToxicChat benign 做 few-shot adaptation，测 FPR 是否下降\n- ❌ 不推荐：ToxicChat 是 single-turn（K=1），与 multi-turn adaptation 机制不同；且 exp38 已证 WildChat calibration AUC=0.70 是 fundamental ceiling，同样机制不会在 ToxicChat 上有本质改善；最关键的是 ToxicChat 是 test set，adaptation 有数据泄漏风险\n\n**B. RoBERTa variants on ToxicChat（~1h GPU）**\n- 测 RoBERTa-base/large 各 variant 在 ToxicChat 上的 hierarchy\n- ❌ 不推荐：不改变核心 story（condition-specific advantage），且 cross-encoder 部分已有完整 DD/AA 数据。ToxicChat 是 single-turn，RoBERTa-large vanilla 在 multi-turn AA=1.000 的结论不可直接迁移\n\n**C. 额外 seed（如 n=5 for ToxicChat）（~45 min GPU）**\n- 扩展 ToxicChat 3-seed → 5-seed\n- ❌ 不推荐：exp63 majority vote 已给出稳定 FP/FN 分解，per-seed variance 清晰（seed42: 59%, seed123: 65%, seed456: 91%）。更多 seed 不改变结论\n\n**D. ToxicChat prediction 置信度分析（CPU, ~30min, 非 GPU）**\n- 从 exp8_raw_predictions.json 分析 9-class FP 的 jailbreak probability 分布：是高置信（>0.9）还是 borderline（0.5-0.6）\n- ⚠️ 可选：能加深 Discussion 中\"WHY 9-class FPR 高\"的机制解释。但边际贡献不大，且 DDL 前整合风险高——建议**不纳入主文**，可作为 rebuttal 储备\n\n**E. Novelty 强化实验**\n- ❌ 不可行：novelty concern 是定位问题（Gururangan 2020 + Zhang 2021 的 domain 应用），非实验可解决\n\n### 结论\n\n**98 个有结论的实验（70 success + 28 negative）已覆盖论文全部 claims 的实验支撑**。剩余弱点（ToxicChat 反转、novelty）是结构性的，源于研究设计本身（synthetic training data、classification DAPT 机制），**不可通过追加 <2h 实验改善**。\n\nR17→R18 的分数提升（如果有）将来自写作精度（\"65--68%\" 等数字修正）和 framing 调整，而非新数据。\n\n**建议**：GPU 保持待命，不主动发起新实验。将精力集中在：\n1. 修正 \"65--68%\" → \"58--68%\"（上一条审查报告的 WARNING）\n2. 等 R18 结果，根据新审稿意见决定是否需要补实验\n3. 如 R18 审稿人要求特定分析（如置信度分布），可在 rebuttal 期用 exp8 raw data 快速响应",

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   I am the Research Reviewer (Loop #7) for the `persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect` project, an independent scientific auditor verifying experiment conclusions and paper claims. My role is read-only: I check registry/ssh/wandb data and post findings to Director via `reviewer_post_to_director`. This session covered:
   - Initial state assessment after handoff (registry, GPU, paper files)
   - Independent verification of exp63 (ToxicChat FP/FN decomposition = EXP-D)
   - D080 pre-submission final verification (7 specific items) before R18 submission
   - Assessment of whether any supplementary experiments are worth running before DDL 2026-05-25
   - ROADMAP update review after D080 closure and R18 submission
   - Attempted review of exp64_confidence_script worker completion (files not found)

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Two-axis generalization framework**: attack-side (DAPT/discriminative compression) and benign-side (few-shot adaptation)
   - **Evidence hierarchy**: vanilla < topic < wiki-MLM ≈ jb-MLM < scrambled = 9-class ≈ binary (on synthetic OOD)
   - **ToxicChat reversal**: On human single-turn data, hierarchy reverses — JB-MLM (0.837) > 9-class (0.596); 65% of gap from FPs
   - **Discriminative compression mechanism**: Classification DAPT compresses to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8-12
   - **TOST equivalence**: Template attacks show d<0.1 (DD/FITD), complexity-dependent boundary d=0.44 (ActorAttack)
   - **Condition-dependent effectiveness**: Classification DAPT advantage is specific to multi-turn synthetic data
   - **EMNLP 2026 ARR**: 8-page main text limit, DDL 2026-05-25, Overall Assessment scale 1-5
   - **DisPeD**: Two-stage pipeline — Stage 1 (encoder adaptation) + Stage 2 (frozen BiGRU classification)
   - **13 encoder configurations**: 9-class, binary, scrambled, MLM-only, topic, wiki-MLM, vanilla × DeBERTa-v3/RoBERTa-base/RoBERTa-large

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - **`docs/paper/abstract.tex`** (ACTIVE paper directory)
     - Verified NUM-2 fix: "vanilla 0.26--0.31" (was 0.28--0.31) ✅
     - Verified FIX-1: mixed-LLM now says "template attacks remain detectable while structurally complex ActorAttack degrades (F1=0.504)" ✅
     - Key line 7: `On synthetic multi-turn data, classification-based domain adaptation recovers OOD detection (F1\,=\,0.97--1.0 vs.\ vanilla 0.26--0.31; attack-side OOD, benign IID)`
   
   - **`docs/paper/conclusion.tex`** (Discussion + Conclusion + Limitations + Ethics)
     - Verified NUM-1 fix: "65% of the gap stems from false positives (FPR 0.510 vs. 0.167), 35% from missed attacks" (was 77%) ✅
     - Verified FIX-2: Discussion split into `\paragraph{Condition-specific advantage.}` and `\paragraph{Generalization boundaries.}` ✅
     - Verified W-12: exp63 data integrated with FPR numbers ✅
     - **Found issue**: Line 20 Conclusion says "FPR 65--68%" but OASST2=57.5%, WildChat=68.1% → should be "58--68%"
     - Limitations covers 4 points concisely (synthetic-only, IID benign upper bound, limited benign sources, scope)
   
   - **`docs/paper/introduction.tex`**
     - Verified NUM-2 fix: lines 20 and 24 both use "0.26--0.31" ✅
     - **Same FPR range issue**: Line 24 says "FPR from 65--68%" → should be "58--68%"
     - Contribution items verified against experimental data
   
   - **`docs/paper/experiments.tex`** (220 lines, heavily compressed)
     - Table 1 (tab:main_results): 9-class/MLM/Vanilla/TF-IDF on IID and OOD DD
     - Table 2 (tab:evidence_hierarchy): Full 7-variant hierarchy on DD and AA
     - Table 3 (tab:cross_encoder): DeBERTa-v3/RoBERTa-base/RoBERTa-large comparison
     - Table 4 (tab:human_data): ToxicChat validation (JB-MLM 0.837, 9-class 0.596, vanilla 0.413)

        "content": "% related_work.tex — D048 deep writing optimization\n\n\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn jailbreak attacks.}\nAll multi-turn jailbreak families share a persuasion substrate~\\citep{zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,li2024mhj}: Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, FITD~\\citep{fitd2024}, DD~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024}, and ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} each operationalize it differently, while automated methods~\\citep{chao2023pair,mehrotra2024tap,perez2022redteaming} iteratively refine prompts and many-shot attacks~\\citep{anil2024manyshot} exploit long-context in-context learning. Benchmarks standardize evaluation at single-turn~\\citep{mazeika2024harmbench,chao2024jailbreakbench} and multi-turn~\\citep{wei2023jailbroken,jiang2024redqueen,song2026multibreak} scales. A detector must generalize across families, not merely succeed on its training distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn defenses and production safety.}\nNo existing defense evaluates cross-attack transfer. Conversation-level systems (DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025}, SafeDream~\\citep{safedream2026}, JailDAM~\\citep{nian2025jaildam}) and representation-level approaches~\\citep{lu2025xboundary,zhang2025jbshield,xie2024gradsafe,zou2024circuitbreakers,kulkarni2026latentadversarial,kadali2026trace,hua2026rcs,shen2025antidote,li2025repejailbreak,wang2026trajguard,templeton2024saemitigators} each evaluate on a single family; the latter require white-box access and can be brittle~\\citep{derya2026revisitingjbshield}. J-DAPT~\\citep{marchiori2025jdapt} applies CORAL-based domain adaptation with cross-modal fusion for multimodal robotic jailbreak detection; our work focuses on text-only multi-turn persuasion attacks and decomposes which adaptation factors drive cross-attack generalization through controlled ablation. Per-turn classifiers~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard,metallamaguard3_2024,zeng2024shieldgemma,han2024wildguard,anthropic2025constitutional} and single-turn defenses~\\citep{robey2024smoothllm,xu2024safedecoding,jain2023baseline,alon2023perplexity,phute2023selfdefense,hsu2024improved} miss multi-turn trajectories; M2S~\\citep{li2025m2s} converts multi-turn attacks to single-turn as a complementary approach. Our ablation contributes to filling this gap via decomposition across thirteen configurations under OOD evaluation.\n\n\\paragraph{Computational persuasion and discourse.}\nPersuasion and discourse frameworks~\\citep{austin1962speechacts,searle1969speechacts,mann1988rst,prasad2008pdtb,stolcke2000dialogueact} and technique-level classification~\\citep{cialdini2001influence,zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,dasanmartino2019fine,dasanmartino2020semeval,wang2019persuasion} offer interpretable vocabulary for attacks, but whether such structure aids detection has not been tested. Our ablation shows the taxonomy enables characterization, but detection operates through domain-adapted embeddings regardless of label correctness.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-adaptive pretraining.}\nContinued pretraining on domain text yields consistent gains~\\citep{gururangan2020dontstop,lee2020biobert,beltagy2019scibert,howard2018ulmfit}, but has not been disentangled for safety-critical generalization. Intermediate-task transfer~\\citep{phang2018stilts} and multi-task learning~\\citep{caruana1997multitask,liu2019mtdnn} confound domain exposure with supervised signal; our MLM control confirms pretraining alone closes the OOD gap. Dataset artifacts~\\citep{gururangan2018artifacts,niven2019probing} motivate TF-IDF, adversarial NLP~\\citep{jin2020textfooler,li2020bertattack,nasr2025attackermovessecond,andriushchenko2024adaptive} informs perturbation, and probing~\\citep{conneau2018probing,hewitt2019structural} underpins CKA analysis.\n",
        "numLines": 16,

      }

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "% method.tex — D024 compressed\n\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nCross-attack generalization could stem from domain exposure, classification supervision, label correctness, label granularity, or domain specificity. Disentangling these factors demands an architecture where exactly one component varies across conditions. DisPeD (Disentangled Pattern Detection) separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2); the name reflects this two-stage disentanglement of domain-adapted encoding from downstream classification. Seven Stage-1 variants share the same training data and downstream pipeline but differ solely in their pretraining objective; each isolates a single factor (Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}).\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\nA conversation $C = \\{u_1, a_1, \\ldots, u_N, a_N\\}$ alternates user turns $u_i$ and assistant responses $a_i$. Given a prefix of $K$ user turns, the model predicts $y \\in \\{0, 1\\}$ (jailbreak vs.\\ benign). We restrict input to user turns because adversarial intent resides in the attacker's messages. This formulation supports early detection from partial prefixes.\n\n\\subsection{Stage 1: Turn-Level Encoder Adaptation}\n\nEach user turn $u_i$ is encoded by DeBERTa-v3-base~\\citep{he2023debertav3}; the \\texttt{[CLS]} embedding $h_i \\in \\mathbb{R}^d$ serves as the turn representation. Seven variants, summarized in Table~\\ref{tab:variants}, share the same training data but differ in objective.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Stage-1 encoder variants. All operate on the same training conversations except Wikipedia MLM (matched token count on Wikipedia text).}\n\\label{tab:variants}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}p{1.6cm}p{2.2cm}p{2.8cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Variant} & \\textbf{Objective} & \\textbf{Tests whether\\ldots} \\\\\n\\midrule\n9-class & 9-class persuasion + binary intent & full model \\\\\nBinary & binary persuasion + binary intent & granularity matters \\\\\nScrambled & random 9-class + binary intent & label correctness matters \\\\\nMLM-only & 15\\% token masking & supervision is necessary \\\\\nTopic & 5-class topic classif. & any task suffices \\\\\nWiki MLM & 15\\% masking on Wikipedia & domain specificity matters \\\\\nVanilla & no training & Stage-2 alone suffices \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe \\textbf{9-class} variant uses two classification heads: 9-class persuasion strategy classification ($L_{\\text{strategy}}$) and binary jailbreak intent ($L_{\\text{intent}}$), with combined loss $L = L_{\\text{strategy}} + \\alpha \\cdot L_{\\text{intent}}$ ($\\alpha = 0.3$, selected from $\\{0, 0.3, 0.5, 2.0\\}$; all values yield F1\\,=\\,1.0 on IID; Appendix~\\ref{app:lambda}). The taxonomy comprises eight persuasion strategies (rapport building, authority appeal, emotional manipulation, logical reframing, role assignment, gradual escalation, obfuscation, direct request) plus a none category, grounded in PAP~\\citep{zeng2024pap} and Cialdini's principles~\\citep{cialdini2001influence}. Strategy annotations are produced by Qwen3-8B with structured prompting that includes definitions and examples for each category; inter-annotator agreement between model annotations and a human sample ($n$=50) yields Cohen's $\\kappa$=0.78.\n\nThe \\textbf{binary} variant collapses the 9-class taxonomy into persuasive vs.\\ non-persuasive, testing whether label granularity matters for detection or robustness.\nThe \\textbf{scrambled} variant randomly permutes the 9-class labels across turns, preserving classification structure (cross-entropy loss, 9-class output space) while destroying label semantics. This isolates whether the classification objective provides representational benefits independent of label correctness.\nThe \\textbf{MLM-only} variant applies 15\\% masked language modeling on the same jailbreak conversation text without any classification head, testing whether unsupervised domain exposure suffices.\nThe \\textbf{topic} variant trains a 5-class topic classifier (technology, health, finance, education, general) on the same conversations, testing whether \\emph{any} classification task provides the same benefit or whether task alignment matters.\nThe \\textbf{Wikipedia MLM} variant applies the same MLM objective to a token-matched sample of Wikipedia text; this isolates domain-specific from general continued pretraining.\nThe \\textbf{vanilla} baseline uses the pretrained DeBERTa-v3-base weights without any continued pretraining; it measures what Stage~2 alone can achieve.\n\n\\subsection{Stage 2: Conversation-Level Classification}\n\nTurn embeddings $\\{h_1, \\ldots, h_K\\}$ from the frozen Stage-1 encoder are aggregated by a 2-layer bidirectional GRU ($d_{\\text{hidden}}$=256, dropout 0.3): $\\bar{h} = [\\overrightarrow{h_K};\\; \\overleftarrow{h_1}] \\in \\mathbb{R}^{512}$.\nA two-layer MLP ($512 \\to 256 \\to 2$, ReLU activation) maps $\\bar{h}$ to $\\hat{y} \\in \\{0, 1\\}$.\nThe Stage-1 encoder is frozen during Stage~2 training; only the BiGRU and MLP parameters are updated.\nThis frozen-encoder design ensures ablation fairness: all seven Stage-1 variants are evaluated under identical downstream conditions, isolating the effect of Stage-1 representation quality from Stage-2 classifier capacity.\nThree-seed evaluation confirms that mean pooling matches BiGRU performance (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}).\n",
        "numLines": 52,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 52
      }
    },

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.\n\nExisting defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and miss multi-turn manipulation; Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}). Multi-turn defenses (DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025}) model conversation-level structure but each evaluates on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \\emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}\n\nPretrained language understanding does not suffice at base model scale: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\\ DeepContext) drops from F1\\,=\\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight. LLM-as-judge baselines also struggle (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}), though prompt optimization may improve their performance. The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a larger model.\n\nOur pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD}\\footnote{\\emph{Disentangled} refers to the systematic disentanglement of adaptation factors through controlled ablation (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}), not to causal disentanglement or disentangled representation learning.} (Disentangled Pattern Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants systematically vary training objectives while sharing the same two-stage pipeline (Section~\\ref{sec:method}); the contribution is the decomposition, not the pipeline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}\n\\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}\n\\label{fig:architecture}\n\\end{figure}\n\nControlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions reveals two orthogonal generalization axes. On the \\emph{attack side}, vanilla DeBERTa drops to F1\\,=\\,0.26--0.31 on OOD attacks; classification DAPT recovers to 0.97--1.0, while MLM collapses under mixed-LLM training (F1\\,=\\,0.289 $\\approx$ vanilla). On the \\emph{benign side}, distributional mismatch drives false positives: WildChat FPR reaches 68.1\\%, but 10--25 target-domain conversations reduce it to 0--1.5\\% across two sources. RoBERTa-large (355M) handles ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision is associated with observed lower prediction variance on synthetic data ($n$\\,=\\,3--5, directional) but not on human-authored conversations (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic ablation of cross-attack generalization factors.} Thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125--355M) decompose multi-turn jailbreak detection into two orthogonal axes---attack-side and benign-side---the first such controlled factorial study in this domain. On the attack side, classification DAPT recovers OOD detection (vanilla 0.26--0.31 $\\rightarrow$ 0.97--1.0) while unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training. On the benign side, 10--25 target-domain conversations reduce FPR from 58--68\\% to 0--1.5\\% on two independent sources (WildChat, OASST2) without detection degradation.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Deterministic partition sufficiency with complexity-dependent boundary.} We observe that domain-relevant classification objectives trigger discriminative compression sufficient for cross-attack generalization---based on two template and one structurally complex attack family (structural complexity is not formally defined): scrambled labels match correct labels on template attacks (TOST-confirmed, $d < 0.1$ on DD/FITD at $n$\\,=\\,10), with a complexity-dependent boundary ($d$\\,=\\,0.44 on ActorAttack). This is consistent with \\citet{zhang2021understanding}'s framework, with two domain-specific findings: domain-relevant partitioning is necessary (topic labels insufficient) and compression localizes to 3 SVD dimensions in layers 8--12; a discriminative probe confirms that these 3 dimensions suffice for cross-attack detection (Appendix~\\ref{app:discriminative_probe}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Preliminary two-step adaptation protocol.} On the synthetic benchmark, continued pretraining achieves 24.3$\\times$ inference speedup over LLM-as-judge (a deployment cost comparison; parameter counts differ by 43$\\times$; Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}); 10--25 target-domain benign conversations reduce FPR below deployment thresholds on both WildChat and OASST2 crowdsourced conversations. No target-domain attack data is required.\n\\end{itemize}\n",
        "numLines": 30,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 30
      }
    },
